People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m here because I can’t seem to stop worrying once something gets in my head. Like, it might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I need to finish, or whether I missed something at work—small things, or not always small, I suppose—but once it starts, I can’t really… put it away.

I’m a librarian, so I keep thinking I should be able to organize it, make a list, shelve it somewhere mentally, you know? I make lists, I try to redirect myself, and then I end up worrying about whether the list is complete. So yes, that’s mostly why I came in.

manifestation: Over this past year my worrying has spread from just my mother’s safety to finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, and whether I’m being a good daughter, and even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel.\n- key: S059\n  name: Concentration_Difficulty\n  description: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.\n  manifestation: During cataloging reviews or staff meetings, I’ll be looking right at the screen or the agenda and suddenly realize I haven’t absorbed a word because my mind has wandered into some problem I think I should have prevented.\n- key: S061\n  name: Muscle_Tension\n  description: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.\n  manifestation: By the end of the day my neck and shoulders feel locked up from bracing myself, and I catch myself clenching my jaw while answering emails or listening for my mother in the next room.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve started missing small but important details at the library and had to hand off parts of a grant presentation after I froze, which is not like me and has made me worry about whether I can keep leading my team well.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and two months, since my mother moved into my home after her fall.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / librarian_or_media_collections_specialist / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S057\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop worrying once something gets in my head. Like, it might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I need to finish, or whether I missed something at work—small things, or not always small, I suppose—but once it starts, I can’t really… put it away.\n\nI’m a librarian, so I keep thinking I should be able to organize it, make a list, shelve it somewhere mentally, you know? I make lists, I try to redirect myself, and then I end up worrying about whether the list is complete. So yes, that’s mostly why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way—like, a sudden peak in minutes. I mean, I do get very worked up sometimes, but it’s more that my mind keeps circling. Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. Once a worry starts, it’s like I can’t put it back on the shelf in my mind.

Yes, mostly. It used to be almost entirely about my mother—whether she was safe, whether I’d missed something she needed—but over this past year it’s sort of… spread.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop worrying once something gets in my head. Like, it might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I need to finish, or whether I missed something at work—small things, or not always small, I suppose—but once it starts, I can’t really… put it away.\n\nI’m a librarian, so I keep thinking I should be able to organize it, make a list, shelve it somewhere mentally, you know? I make lists, I try to redirect myself, and then I end up worrying about whether the list is complete. So yes, that’s mostly why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way—like, a sudden peak in minutes. I don’t think I’ve had that specific kind of sudden intense fear you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Which sounds odd, maybe, but I’ll be typing and then notice my teeth are pressed together. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. Once a worry starts, it’s like I can’t put it back on the shelf in my mind.\n\nIt might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report at work, or just wondering if I missed something important in a conversation. I’ll make lists, I’ll try to redirect myself, I’ll tell myself, “You’ve already thought this through,” but it just keeps coming back around. Sometimes I’ll be doing something completely different and realize I’m still mentally checking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about everyday, routine things like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. It used to be almost entirely about my mother—whether she was safe, whether I’d missed something she needed—but over this past year it’s sort of… spread.\n\nNow it’s finances, decisions at work with staff, deadlines, the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter. Even small things can feel like they’re about to unravel. I mean, I keep wanting to say it’s not catastrophic, because I’m still going to work and getting things done, mostly. But it is starting to show up in ways that scare me a little.\n\nAt the library I’ve missed some small details—nothing huge, but things I normally catch. And there was a grant presentation recently where I just… froze. I had to hand parts of it off to someone else, which was embarrassing, and not really like me. Yes… I do. That’s the frustrating part, because even when there isn’t an actual crisis, my mind sort of goes looking for one.

Over this past year it’s spread from worrying mostly about my mother’s safety to all these other things—money, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. And sometimes everything is technically fine, like the bills are paid or work is moving along, but one small thing can still make me feel like it’s all about to unravel.

I’ll think, “Why am I worrying right now? Nothing is happening.” But then I’ll find something—an email, a repair I haven’t scheduled, something someone said—and it starts up again.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop worrying once something gets in my head. I make lists, I try to redirect myself, and then I end up worrying about whether the list is complete. So yes, that’s mostly why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way—like, a sudden peak in minutes. I mean, I do get very worked up sometimes, but it’s more that my mind keeps circling. Although “sudden” is tricky, isn’t it, because sometimes you only notice it once it’s already taken over.\n\nSorry, I’m probably over-answering. I don’t think I’ve had that specific kind of sudden intense fear you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Which sounds odd, maybe, but I’ll be typing and then notice my teeth are pressed together. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. I mean, I keep wanting to say it’s not catastrophic, because I’m still going to work and getting things done, mostly. And it’s distressing because my work has always been the place where I felt competent.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things even when everything is going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s the frustrating part, because even when there isn’t an actual crisis, my mind sort of goes looking for one.\n\nOver this past year it’s spread from worrying mostly about my mother’s safety to all these other things—money, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. And sometimes everything is technically fine, like the bills are paid or work is moving along, but one small thing can still make me feel like it’s all about to unravel.\n\nI’ll think, “Why am I worrying right now? Once a worry starts, it’s like I can’t put it back on the shelf in my mind.\n\nIt might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report at work, or just wondering if I missed something important in a conversation. I’ll make lists, I’ll try to redirect myself, I’ll tell myself, “You’ve already thought this through,” but it just keeps coming back around. Sometimes I’ll be doing something completely different and realize I’m still mentally checking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about everyday, routine things like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. It used to be almost entirely about my mother—whether she was safe, whether I’d missed something she needed—but over this past year it’s sort of… spread.\n\nNow it’s finances, decisions at work with staff, deadlines, the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter. Even small things can feel like they’re about to unravel. Like one email or one bill isn’t just that one thing, it turns into this whole chain of “what if I’ve missed something?” or “what if this causes a bigger problem?” And then I’m off thinking about ten other things, which is probably not what you asked exactly, but yes, it’s everyday things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I keep wanting to say it’s not catastrophic, because I’m still going to work and getting things done, mostly. But it is starting to show up in ways that scare me a little.\n\nAt the library I’ve missed some small details—nothing huge, but things I normally catch. And there was a grant presentation recently where I just… froze. I had to hand parts of it off to someone else, which was embarrassing, and not really like me. I kept thinking afterward about whether everyone noticed, and whether I can still lead the team properly.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering a bit. But yes, it’s interfering. And it’s distressing because my work has always been the place where I felt competent.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things even when everything is going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s the frustrating part, because even when there isn’t an actual crisis, my mind sort of goes looking for one.\n\nOver this past year it’s spread from worrying mostly about my mother’s safety to all these other things—money, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. And sometimes everything is technically fine, like the bills are paid or work is moving along, but one small thing can still make me feel like it’s all about to unravel.\n\nI’ll think, “Why am I worrying right now? Nothing is happening.” But then I’ll find something—an email, a repair I haven’t scheduled, something someone said—and it starts up again.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change with friends or family in a clear way.\n\nI mean, with family it’s hard to separate what’s “worry affecting the relationship” from just… normal family obligations, if that makes sense. And with friends, I suppose I may be a little less present sometimes, but I don’t know if they would say anything is different.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering very directly. I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it affecting relationships in a major way, at least not that I can point to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel very wound up or overwhelmed, but “intense fear that came on suddenly” makes me think of something very dramatic, and I don’t know if that’s what you mean.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. It’s like they pile on top of each other.

At first it was mostly my mother’s safety, and I could sort of tell myself, “Well, that makes sense, she’s my mother.” But over this past year it’s spread into all these different buckets—money, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter.

And they don’t stay separate, either. I’ll be thinking about a work deadline, and then suddenly I remember a house repair, and then I’m worrying about money, and then I’m back to my mother again. It’s almost embarrassing, because sometimes it’s just one small thing, and my brain treats it like everything is about to unravel.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop worrying once something gets in my head. Like, it might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I need to finish, or whether I missed something at work—small things, or not always small, I suppose—but once it starts, I can’t really… put it away.\n\nI’m a librarian, so I keep thinking I should be able to organize it, make a list, shelve it somewhere mentally, you know? I make lists, I try to redirect myself, and then I end up worrying about whether the list is complete. So yes, that’s mostly why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way—like, a sudden peak in minutes. I mean, I do get very worked up sometimes, but it’s more that my mind keeps circling. Although “sudden” is tricky, isn’t it, because sometimes you only notice it once it’s already taken over.\n\nSorry, I’m probably over-answering. I don’t think I’ve had that specific kind of sudden intense fear you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I avoid things in a clear, deliberate way, like “I can’t go there” or anything. It’s more… I might put something off because I’m thinking about it too much, but then I’ll still usually do it.\n\nAlthough now I’m thinking about the staff meetings at work, because sometimes I sit there rehearsing what I’m going to say instead of listening, and then I worry I’ve missed the point—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. I guess I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding whole situations because of the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tension, restlessness, or fatigue when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes—mostly tension, I think. By the end of the day my neck and shoulders feel sort of locked up, like I’ve been bracing for something without realizing it.\n\nAnd I catch myself clenching my jaw a lot—when I’m answering emails, especially, or if I’m listening for my mother in the next room. Which sounds odd, maybe, but I’ll be typing and then notice my teeth are pressed together. Then of course I start wondering how long I’ve been doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. Once a worry starts, it’s like I can’t put it back on the shelf in my mind.\n\nIt might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report at work, or just wondering if I missed something important in a conversation. I’ll make lists, I’ll try to redirect myself, I’ll tell myself, “You’ve already thought this through,” but it just keeps coming back around. Sometimes I’ll be doing something completely different and realize I’m still mentally checking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about everyday, routine things like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. It used to be almost entirely about my mother—whether she was safe, whether I’d missed something she needed—but over this past year it’s sort of… spread.\n\nNow it’s finances, decisions at work with staff, deadlines, the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter. Even small things can feel like they’re about to unravel. Like one email or one bill isn’t just that one thing, it turns into this whole chain of “what if I’ve missed something?” or “what if this causes a bigger problem?” And then I’m off thinking about ten other things, which is probably not what you asked exactly, but yes, it’s everyday things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I keep wanting to say it’s not catastrophic, because I’m still going to work and getting things done, mostly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it does, especially at work. That’s the part that bothers me most, because I’ve always been pretty dependable at the library. I’m usually the person who catches the small things, keeps the collections details straight, remembers which committee needs what.

Lately I’ve missed these small but important details, and then I start worrying about the fact that I missed them, which probably doesn’t help. And there was this grant presentation—I had to hand off parts of it because I just sort of froze. That’s really not like me.

I don’t know if it affects every single area equally. Like with relationships, I’m less sure. But work, definitely. And it makes me worry whether I’m still doing a good job leading my team, which feels awful to even say.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop worrying once something gets in my head. Like, it might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I need to finish, or whether I missed something at work—small things, or not always small, I suppose—but once it starts, I can’t really… put it away.\n\nI’m a librarian, so I keep thinking I should be able to organize it, make a list, shelve it somewhere mentally, you know? I make lists, I try to redirect myself, and then I end up worrying about whether the list is complete. So yes, that’s mostly why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way—like, a sudden peak in minutes. I mean, I do get very worked up sometimes, but it’s more that my mind keeps circling. Although “sudden” is tricky, isn’t it, because sometimes you only notice it once it’s already taken over.\n\nSorry, I’m probably over-answering. I don’t think I’ve had that specific kind of sudden intense fear you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I avoid things in a clear, deliberate way, like “I can’t go there” or anything. It’s more… I might put something off because I’m thinking about it too much, but then I’ll still usually do it.\n\nAlthough now I’m thinking about the staff meetings at work, because sometimes I sit there rehearsing what I’m going to say instead of listening, and then I worry I’ve missed the point—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. I guess I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding whole situations because of the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tension, restlessness, or fatigue when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes—mostly tension, I think. By the end of the day my neck and shoulders feel sort of locked up, like I’ve been bracing for something without realizing it.\n\nAnd I catch myself clenching my jaw a lot—when I’m answering emails, especially, or if I’m listening for my mother in the next room. Which sounds odd, maybe, but I’ll be typing and then notice my teeth are pressed together. Then of course I start wondering how long I’ve been doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. Once a worry starts, it’s like I can’t put it back on the shelf in my mind.\n\nIt might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report at work, or just wondering if I missed something important in a conversation. I’ll make lists, I’ll try to redirect myself, I’ll tell myself, “You’ve already thought this through,” but it just keeps coming back around. Sometimes I’ll be doing something completely different and realize I’m still mentally checking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about everyday, routine things like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. It used to be almost entirely about my mother—whether she was safe, whether I’d missed something she needed—but over this past year it’s sort of… spread.\n\nNow it’s finances, decisions at work with staff, deadlines, the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter. Even small things can feel like they’re about to unravel. Like one email or one bill isn’t just that one thing, it turns into this whole chain of “what if I’ve missed something?” or “what if this causes a bigger problem?” And then I’m off thinking about ten other things, which is probably not what you asked exactly, but yes, it’s everyday things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I keep wanting to say it’s not catastrophic, because I’m still going to work and getting things done, mostly. That’s the frustrating part, because even when there isn’t an actual crisis, my mind sort of goes looking for one.\n\nOver this past year it’s spread from worrying mostly about my mother’s safety to all these other things—money, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Nothing is happening.” But then I’ll find something—an email, a repair I haven’t scheduled, something someone said—and it starts up again.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel very wound up or overwhelmed, but “intense fear that came on suddenly” makes me think of something very dramatic, and I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike, sometimes I can get thrown off by an email or something unexpected at work, and then my thoughts start going, but that feels more like it builds from a worry rather than appearing out of nowhere. Sorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I guess I haven’t really noticed a sudden episode like that, at least not one I’d clearly identify that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about multiple things at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s like they pile on top of each other.\n\nAt first it was mostly my mother’s safety, and I could sort of tell myself, “Well, that makes sense, she’s my mother.” But over this past year it’s spread into all these different buckets—money, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter.\n\nAnd they don’t stay separate, either. I’ll be thinking about a work deadline, and then suddenly I remember a house repair, and then I’m worrying about money, and then I’m back to my mother again. It’s almost embarrassing, because sometimes it’s just one small thing, and my brain treats it like everything is about to unravel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying affects most areas of your life, such as work, relationships, and personal activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, especially at work. That’s the part that bothers me most, because I’ve always been pretty dependable at the library. I’m usually the person who catches the small things, keeps the collections details straight, remembers which committee needs what.\n\nLately I’ve missed these small but important details, and then I start worrying about the fact that I missed them, which probably doesn’t help. And there was this grant presentation—I had to hand off parts of it because I just sort of froze. That’s really not like me.\n\nI don’t know if it affects every single area equally. Like with relationships, I’m less sure. But work, definitely. Yes… almost every day, I think. It’s not always the exact same worry, which is part of what makes it feel so exhausting.

At first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and I could almost justify that to myself. But over the past year it’s spread into finances, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things with the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and then one little thing can set off this whole chain. Like I’ll notice a bill on the counter, and suddenly I’m thinking about the roof, and work coverage, and whether I should have called my mother earlier.

So yes, I’d say it’s there most days. Even when there isn’t one big obvious thing happening, my mind seems to find something to pull at.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop worrying once something gets in my head. Like, it might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I need to finish, or whether I missed something at work—small things, or not always small, I suppose—but once it starts, I can’t really… put it away.\n\nI’m a librarian, so I keep thinking I should be able to organize it, make a list, shelve it somewhere mentally, you know? I make lists, I try to redirect myself, and then I end up worrying about whether the list is complete. So yes, that’s mostly why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way—like, a sudden peak in minutes. I mean, I do get very worked up sometimes, but it’s more that my mind keeps circling. Although “sudden” is tricky, isn’t it, because sometimes you only notice it once it’s already taken over.\n\nSorry, I’m probably over-answering. I don’t think I’ve had that specific kind of sudden intense fear you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I avoid things in a clear, deliberate way, like “I can’t go there” or anything. It’s more… I might put something off because I’m thinking about it too much, but then I’ll still usually do it.\n\nAlthough now I’m thinking about the staff meetings at work, because sometimes I sit there rehearsing what I’m going to say instead of listening, and then I worry I’ve missed the point—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. I guess I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding whole situations because of the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tension, restlessness, or fatigue when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes—mostly tension, I think. By the end of the day my neck and shoulders feel sort of locked up, like I’ve been bracing for something without realizing it.\n\nAnd I catch myself clenching my jaw a lot—when I’m answering emails, especially, or if I’m listening for my mother in the next room. Which sounds odd, maybe, but I’ll be typing and then notice my teeth are pressed together. Then of course I start wondering how long I’ve been doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. Once a worry starts, it’s like I can’t put it back on the shelf in my mind.\n\nIt might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report at work, or just wondering if I missed something important in a conversation. I’ll make lists, I’ll try to redirect myself, I’ll tell myself, “You’ve already thought this through,” but it just keeps coming back around. Sometimes I’ll be doing something completely different and realize I’m still mentally checking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about everyday, routine things like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. It used to be almost entirely about my mother—whether she was safe, whether I’d missed something she needed—but over this past year it’s sort of… spread.\n\nNow it’s finances, decisions at work with staff, deadlines, the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter. Even small things can feel like they’re about to unravel. Like one email or one bill isn’t just that one thing, it turns into this whole chain of “what if I’ve missed something?” or “what if this causes a bigger problem?” And then I’m off thinking about ten other things, which is probably not what you asked exactly, but yes, it’s everyday things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I keep wanting to say it’s not catastrophic, because I’m still going to work and getting things done, mostly. That’s the frustrating part, because even when there isn’t an actual crisis, my mind sort of goes looking for one.\n\nOver this past year it’s spread from worrying mostly about my mother’s safety to all these other things—money, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. And sometimes everything is technically fine, like the bills are paid or work is moving along, but one small thing can still make me feel like it’s all about to unravel.\n\nI’ll think, “Why am I worrying right now? Nothing is happening.” But then I’ll find something—an email, a repair I haven’t scheduled, something someone said—and it starts up again.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Even small things can feel like they’re about to unravel. Like one email or one bill isn’t just that one thing, it turns into this whole chain of “what if I’ve missed something?” or “what if this causes a bigger problem?” And then I’m off thinking about ten other things, which is probably not what you asked exactly, but yes, it’s everyday things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I keep wanting to say it’s not catastrophic, because I’m still going to work and getting things done, mostly. But it is starting to show up in ways that scare me a little.\n\nAt the library I’ve missed some small details—nothing huge, but things I normally catch. And there was a grant presentation recently where I just… froze. I had to hand parts of it off to someone else, which was embarrassing, and not really like me. I kept thinking afterward about whether everyone noticed, and whether I can still lead the team properly.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering a bit. But yes, it’s interfering. And it’s distressing because my work has always been the place where I felt competent.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things even when everything is going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s the frustrating part, because even when there isn’t an actual crisis, my mind sort of goes looking for one.\n\nOver this past year it’s spread from worrying mostly about my mother’s safety to all these other things—money, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. And sometimes everything is technically fine, like the bills are paid or work is moving along, but one small thing can still make me feel like it’s all about to unravel.\n\nI’ll think, “Why am I worrying right now? Nothing is happening.” But then I’ll find something—an email, a repair I haven’t scheduled, something someone said—and it starts up again.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change with friends or family in a clear way.\n\nI mean, with family it’s hard to separate what’s “worry affecting the relationship” from just… normal family obligations, if that makes sense. And with friends, I suppose I may be a little less present sometimes, but I don’t know if they would say anything is different.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering very directly. I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it affecting relationships in a major way, at least not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that came on suddenly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel very wound up or overwhelmed, but “intense fear that came on suddenly” makes me think of something very dramatic, and I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike, sometimes I can get thrown off by an email or something unexpected at work, and then my thoughts start going, but that feels more like it builds from a worry rather than appearing out of nowhere. Sorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I guess I haven’t really noticed a sudden episode like that, at least not one I’d clearly identify that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about multiple things at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s like they pile on top of each other.\n\nAt first it was mostly my mother’s safety, and I could sort of tell myself, “Well, that makes sense, she’s my mother.” But over this past year it’s spread into all these different buckets—money, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter.\n\nAnd they don’t stay separate, either. I’ll be thinking about a work deadline, and then suddenly I remember a house repair, and then I’m worrying about money, and then I’m back to my mother again. It’s almost embarrassing, because sometimes it’s just one small thing, and my brain treats it like everything is about to unravel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying affects most areas of your life, such as work, relationships, and personal activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, especially at work. That’s the part that bothers me most, because I’ve always been pretty dependable at the library. I’m usually the person who catches the small things, keeps the collections details straight, remembers which committee needs what.\n\nLately I’ve missed these small but important details, and then I start worrying about the fact that I missed them, which probably doesn’t help. And there was this grant presentation—I had to hand off parts of it because I just sort of froze. That’s really not like me.\n\nI don’t know if it affects every single area equally. Like with relationships, I’m less sure. But work, definitely. And it makes me worry whether I’m still doing a good job leading my team, which feels awful to even say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… almost every day, I think. It’s not always the exact same worry, which is part of what makes it feel so exhausting.\n\nAt first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and I could almost justify that to myself. But over the past year it’s spread into finances, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things with the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and then one little thing can set off this whole chain. Like I’ll notice a bill on the counter, and suddenly I’m thinking about the roof, and work coverage, and whether I should have called my mother earlier.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most days. Even when there isn’t one big obvious thing happening, my mind seems to find something to pull at.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop worrying once something gets in my head. Like, it might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I need to finish, or whether I missed something at work—small things, or not always small, I suppose—but once it starts, I can’t really… put it away.\n\nI’m a librarian, so I keep thinking I should be able to organize it, make a list, shelve it somewhere mentally, you know? I make lists, I try to redirect myself, and then I end up worrying about whether the list is complete. So yes, that’s mostly why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way—like, a sudden peak in minutes. I mean, I do get very worked up sometimes, but it’s more that my mind keeps circling. Although “sudden” is tricky, isn’t it, because sometimes you only notice it once it’s already taken over.\n\nSorry, I’m probably over-answering. I don’t think I’ve had that specific kind of sudden intense fear you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I avoid things in a clear, deliberate way, like “I can’t go there” or anything. It’s more… I might put something off because I’m thinking about it too much, but then I’ll still usually do it.\n\nAlthough now I’m thinking about the staff meetings at work, because sometimes I sit there rehearsing what I’m going to say instead of listening, and then I worry I’ve missed the point—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. I guess I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding whole situations because of the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tension, restlessness, or fatigue when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes—mostly tension, I think. By the end of the day my neck and shoulders feel sort of locked up, like I’ve been bracing for something without realizing it.\n\nAnd I catch myself clenching my jaw a lot—when I’m answering emails, especially, or if I’m listening for my mother in the next room. Which sounds odd, maybe, but I’ll be typing and then notice my teeth are pressed together. Then of course I start wondering how long I’ve been doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. Once a worry starts, it’s like I can’t put it back on the shelf in my mind.\n\nIt might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report at work, or just wondering if I missed something important in a conversation. I’ll make lists, I’ll try to redirect myself, I’ll tell myself, “You’ve already thought this through,” but it just keeps coming back around. Sometimes I’ll be doing something completely different and realize I’m still mentally checking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about everyday, routine things like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. It used to be almost entirely about my mother—whether she was safe, whether I’d missed something she needed—but over this past year it’s sort of… spread.\n\nNow it’s finances, decisions at work with staff, deadlines, the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter. Even small things can feel like they’re about to unravel. Like one email or one bill isn’t just that one thing, it turns into this whole chain of “what if I’ve missed something?” or “what if this causes a bigger problem?” And then I’m off thinking about ten other things, which is probably not what you asked exactly, but yes, it’s everyday things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I keep wanting to say it’s not catastrophic, because I’m still going to work and getting things done, mostly. But it is starting to show up in ways that scare me a little.\n\nAt the library I’ve missed some small details—nothing huge, but things I normally catch. And there was a grant presentation recently where I just… froze. I had to hand parts of it off to someone else, which was embarrassing, and not really like me. I kept thinking afterward about whether everyone noticed, and whether I can still lead the team properly.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering a bit. But yes, it’s interfering. And it’s distressing because my work has always been the place where I felt competent.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things even when everything is going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s the frustrating part, because even when there isn’t an actual crisis, my mind sort of goes looking for one.\n\nOver this past year it’s spread from worrying mostly about my mother’s safety to all these other things—money, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. And sometimes everything is technically fine, like the bills are paid or work is moving along, but one small thing can still make me feel like it’s all about to unravel.\n\nI’ll think, “Why am I worrying right now? Nothing is happening.” But then I’ll find something—an email, a repair I haven’t scheduled, something someone said—and it starts up again.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change with friends or family in a clear way.\n\nI mean, with family it’s hard to separate what’s “worry affecting the relationship” from just… normal family obligations, if that makes sense. And with friends, I suppose I may be a little less present sometimes, but I don’t know if they would say anything is different.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering very directly. I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it affecting relationships in a major way, at least not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that came on suddenly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel very wound up or overwhelmed, but “intense fear that came on suddenly” makes me think of something very dramatic, and I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike, sometimes I can get thrown off by an email or something unexpected at work, and then my thoughts start going, but that feels more like it builds from a worry rather than appearing out of nowhere. Sorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I guess I haven’t really noticed a sudden episode like that, at least not one I’d clearly identify that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about multiple things at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s like they pile on top of each other.\n\nAt first it was mostly my mother’s safety, and I could sort of tell myself, “Well, that makes sense, she’s my mother.” But over this past year it’s spread into all these different buckets—money, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter.\n\nAnd they don’t stay separate, either. I’ll be thinking about a work deadline, and then suddenly I remember a house repair, and then I’m worrying about money, and then I’m back to my mother again. It’s almost embarrassing, because sometimes it’s just one small thing, and my brain treats it like everything is about to unravel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying affects most areas of your life, such as work, relationships, and personal activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, especially at work. That’s the part that bothers me most, because I’ve always been pretty dependable at the library. I’m usually the person who catches the small things, keeps the collections details straight, remembers which committee needs what.\n\nLately I’ve missed these small but important details, and then I start worrying about the fact that I missed them, which probably doesn’t help. And there was this grant presentation—I had to hand off parts of it because I just sort of froze. That’s really not like me.\n\nI don’t know if it affects every single area equally. Like with relationships, I’m less sure. But work, definitely. And it makes me worry whether I’m still doing a good job leading my team, which feels awful to even say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… almost every day, I think. It’s not always the exact same worry, which is part of what makes it feel so exhausting.\n\nAt first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and I could almost justify that to myself. But over the past year it’s spread into finances, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things with the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and then one little thing can set off this whole chain. Like I’ll notice a bill on the counter, and suddenly I’m thinking about the roof, and work coverage, and whether I should have called my mother earlier.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most days. Even when there isn’t one big obvious thing happening, my mind seems to find something to pull at.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure what counts as one, honestly. I’ve heard people describe them in different ways, and sometimes I think, well, everyone uses that phrase now—like at work someone will say they “had a panic attack” over a printer jam, which is not the same thing, I know.\n\nI don’t think I’ve had something I’d clearly call a panic attack, though. I get very worked up and overwhelmed, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… that’s probably the main reason I came in, honestly. Once a worry starts, I can’t seem to put it away.

Yes… I think it is excessive, even though part of me keeps trying to argue that it’s all “reasonable.” I mean, my mother’s safety is a real concern, and work deadlines are real, and bills are real. It’s not like I’m inventing things out of nowhere.

Although “sudden” is tricky, isn’t it, because sometimes you only notice it once it’s already taken over.\n\nSorry, I’m probably over-answering. I don’t think I avoid things in a clear, deliberate way, like “I can’t go there” or anything. It’s more… I might put something off because I’m thinking about it too much, but then I’ll still usually do it.\n\nAlthough now I’m thinking about the staff meetings at work, because sometimes I sit there rehearsing what I’m going to say instead of listening, and then I worry I’ve missed the point—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. I guess I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding whole situations because of the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tension, restlessness, or fatigue when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes—mostly tension, I think. By the end of the day my neck and shoulders feel sort of locked up, like I’ve been bracing for something without realizing it.\n\nAnd I catch myself clenching my jaw a lot—when I’m answering emails, especially, or if I’m listening for my mother in the next room. Which sounds odd, maybe, but I’ll be typing and then notice my teeth are pressed together. Then of course I start wondering how long I’ve been doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. Once a worry starts, it’s like I can’t put it back on the shelf in my mind.\n\nIt might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report at work, or just wondering if I missed something important in a conversation. I’ll make lists, I’ll try to redirect myself, I’ll tell myself, “You’ve already thought this through,” but it just keeps coming back around. Sometimes I’ll be doing something completely different and realize I’m still mentally checking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about everyday, routine things like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. It used to be almost entirely about my mother—whether she was safe, whether I’d missed something she needed—but over this past year it’s sort of… spread.\n\nNow it’s finances, decisions at work with staff, deadlines, the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter. Even small things can feel like they’re about to unravel. Like one email or one bill isn’t just that one thing, it turns into this whole chain of “what if I’ve missed something?” or “what if this causes a bigger problem?” And then I’m off thinking about ten other things, which is probably not what you asked exactly, but yes, it’s everyday things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I keep wanting to say it’s not catastrophic, because I’m still going to work and getting things done, mostly. But it is starting to show up in ways that scare me a little.\n\nAt the library I’ve missed some small details—nothing huge, but things I normally catch. And there was a grant presentation recently where I just… froze. I had to hand parts of it off to someone else, which was embarrassing, and not really like me. I kept thinking afterward about whether everyone noticed, and whether I can still lead the team properly.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering a bit. But yes, it’s interfering. And it’s distressing because my work has always been the place where I felt competent.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things even when everything is going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s the frustrating part, because even when there isn’t an actual crisis, my mind sort of goes looking for one.\n\nOver this past year it’s spread from worrying mostly about my mother’s safety to all these other things—money, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. And sometimes everything is technically fine, like the bills are paid or work is moving along, but one small thing can still make me feel like it’s all about to unravel.\n\nI’ll think, “Why am I worrying right now? Nothing is happening.” But then I’ll find something—an email, a repair I haven’t scheduled, something someone said—and it starts up again.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change with friends or family in a clear way.\n\nI mean, with family it’s hard to separate what’s “worry affecting the relationship” from just… normal family obligations, if that makes sense. And with friends, I suppose I may be a little less present sometimes, but I don’t know if they would say anything is different.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering very directly. I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it affecting relationships in a major way, at least not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that came on suddenly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel very wound up or overwhelmed, but “intense fear that came on suddenly” makes me think of something very dramatic, and I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike, sometimes I can get thrown off by an email or something unexpected at work, and then my thoughts start going, but that feels more like it builds from a worry rather than appearing out of nowhere. Sorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I guess I haven’t really noticed a sudden episode like that, at least not one I’d clearly identify that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about multiple things at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s like they pile on top of each other.\n\nAt first it was mostly my mother’s safety, and I could sort of tell myself, “Well, that makes sense, she’s my mother.” But over this past year it’s spread into all these different buckets—money, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter.\n\nAnd they don’t stay separate, either. I’ll be thinking about a work deadline, and then suddenly I remember a house repair, and then I’m worrying about money, and then I’m back to my mother again. It’s almost embarrassing, because sometimes it’s just one small thing, and my brain treats it like everything is about to unravel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying affects most areas of your life, such as work, relationships, and personal activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, especially at work. That’s the part that bothers me most, because I’ve always been pretty dependable at the library. I’m usually the person who catches the small things, keeps the collections details straight, remembers which committee needs what.\n\nLately I’ve missed these small but important details, and then I start worrying about the fact that I missed them, which probably doesn’t help. And there was this grant presentation—I had to hand off parts of it because I just sort of froze. That’s really not like me.\n\nI don’t know if it affects every single area equally. Like with relationships, I’m less sure. But work, definitely. And it makes me worry whether I’m still doing a good job leading my team, which feels awful to even say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… almost every day, I think. It’s not always the exact same worry, which is part of what makes it feel so exhausting.\n\nAt first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and I could almost justify that to myself. But over the past year it’s spread into finances, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things with the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and then one little thing can set off this whole chain. Like I’ll notice a bill on the counter, and suddenly I’m thinking about the roof, and work coverage, and whether I should have called my mother earlier.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most days. Even when there isn’t one big obvious thing happening, my mind seems to find something to pull at.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure what counts as one, honestly. I’ve heard people describe them in different ways, and sometimes I think, well, everyone uses that phrase now—like at work someone will say they “had a panic attack” over a printer jam, which is not the same thing, I know.\n\nI don’t think I’ve had something I’d clearly call a panic attack, though. I get very worked up and overwhelmed, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the main reason I came in, honestly. Once a worry starts, I can’t seem to put it away.\n\nIt can be about my mother getting up at night, or some grant report, or whether I missed some small thing at work—and I’ll try to make a list, or tell myself, “Okay, you’ve handled this,” but it doesn’t really stop. I keep coming back to it, like when a book is mis-shelved and you know it’s somewhere in the stacks but you can’t stop looking for it.\n\nSorry, that’s a library comparison, but… yes. Most of the time, it feels like I can’t control it the way I should be able to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is excessive, considering the actual situations you're worrying about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is excessive, even though part of me keeps trying to argue that it’s all “reasonable.” I mean, my mother’s safety is a real concern, and work deadlines are real, and bills are real. It’s not like I’m inventing things out of nowhere.\n\nBut over this past year it’s spread so much. It used to be mostly about my mother, and now it’s finances, staff decisions, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and then something small, like noticing an email I forgot to answer, can make it feel like everything is about to unravel. So yes, when I step back, the amount of worry is bigger than the actual situation.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I get very worked up and overwhelmed, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the main reason I came in, honestly. Once a worry starts, I can’t seem to put it away.\n\nIt can be about my mother getting up at night, or some grant report, or whether I missed some small thing at work—and I’ll try to make a list, or tell myself, “Okay, you’ve handled this,” but it doesn’t really stop. I keep coming back to it, like when a book is mis-shelved and you know it’s somewhere in the stacks but you can’t stop looking for it.\n\nSorry, that’s a library comparison, but… yes. Most of the time, it feels like I can’t control it the way I should be able to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is excessive, considering the actual situations you're worrying about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is excessive, even though part of me keeps trying to argue that it’s all “reasonable.” I mean, my mother’s safety is a real concern, and work deadlines are real, and bills are real. It’s not like I’m inventing things out of nowhere.\n\nBut over this past year it’s spread so much. It used to be mostly about my mother, and now it’s finances, staff decisions, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and then something small, like noticing an email I forgot to answer, can make it feel like everything is about to unravel. So yes, when I step back, the amount of worry is bigger than the actual situation.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually worry about the same things or does it shift between different areas of your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / librarian_or_media_collections_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"Over this past year my worrying has spread from just my mother’s safety to finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, and whether I’m being a good daughter, and even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It shifts, definitely. It used to be mostly my mother—whether she was safe, whether I should be doing more, that sort of thing. But over this past year it’s kind of… branched out.

Now it can be money, or a staff decision at work, or a deadline, or something with the house. And then I’ll be rinsing a mug or shelving something in my head—sorry, that’s not quite the point—and suddenly I’m thinking, “Did I answer that email?” and it feels like that one small thing means everything is about to unravel.

So yes, it moves around between different parts of my life. It doesn’t stay in one lane anymore.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop worrying once something gets in my head. Like, it might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I need to finish, or whether I missed something at work—small things, or not always small, I suppose—but once it starts, I can’t really… put it away.\n\nI’m a librarian, so I keep thinking I should be able to organize it, make a list, shelve it somewhere mentally, you know? I make lists, I try to redirect myself, and then I end up worrying about whether the list is complete. So yes, that’s mostly why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way—like, a sudden peak in minutes. I mean, I do get very worked up sometimes, but it’s more that my mind keeps circling. Although “sudden” is tricky, isn’t it, because sometimes you only notice it once it’s already taken over.\n\nSorry, I’m probably over-answering. I don’t think I’ve had that specific kind of sudden intense fear you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I avoid things in a clear, deliberate way, like “I can’t go there” or anything. It’s more… I might put something off because I’m thinking about it too much, but then I’ll still usually do it.\n\nAlthough now I’m thinking about the staff meetings at work, because sometimes I sit there rehearsing what I’m going to say instead of listening, and then I worry I’ve missed the point—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. I guess I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding whole situations because of the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tension, restlessness, or fatigue when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes—mostly tension, I think. By the end of the day my neck and shoulders feel sort of locked up, like I’ve been bracing for something without realizing it.\n\nAnd I catch myself clenching my jaw a lot—when I’m answering emails, especially, or if I’m listening for my mother in the next room. Which sounds odd, maybe, but I’ll be typing and then notice my teeth are pressed together. Then of course I start wondering how long I’ve been doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. Once a worry starts, it’s like I can’t put it back on the shelf in my mind.\n\nIt might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report at work, or just wondering if I missed something important in a conversation. I’ll make lists, I’ll try to redirect myself, I’ll tell myself, “You’ve already thought this through,” but it just keeps coming back around. Sometimes I’ll be doing something completely different and realize I’m still mentally checking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about everyday, routine things like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. It used to be almost entirely about my mother—whether she was safe, whether I’d missed something she needed—but over this past year it’s sort of… spread.\n\nNow it’s finances, decisions at work with staff, deadlines, the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter. Even small things can feel like they’re about to unravel. Like one email or one bill isn’t just that one thing, it turns into this whole chain of “what if I’ve missed something?” or “what if this causes a bigger problem?” And then I’m off thinking about ten other things, which is probably not what you asked exactly, but yes, it’s everyday things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I keep wanting to say it’s not catastrophic, because I’m still going to work and getting things done, mostly. But it is starting to show up in ways that scare me a little.\n\nAt the library I’ve missed some small details—nothing huge, but things I normally catch. And there was a grant presentation recently where I just… froze. I had to hand parts of it off to someone else, which was embarrassing, and not really like me. I kept thinking afterward about whether everyone noticed, and whether I can still lead the team properly.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering a bit. But yes, it’s interfering. And it’s distressing because my work has always been the place where I felt competent.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things even when everything is going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s the frustrating part, because even when there isn’t an actual crisis, my mind sort of goes looking for one.\n\nOver this past year it’s spread from worrying mostly about my mother’s safety to all these other things—money, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. And sometimes everything is technically fine, like the bills are paid or work is moving along, but one small thing can still make me feel like it’s all about to unravel.\n\nI’ll think, “Why am I worrying right now? Nothing is happening.” But then I’ll find something—an email, a repair I haven’t scheduled, something someone said—and it starts up again.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change with friends or family in a clear way.\n\nI mean, with family it’s hard to separate what’s “worry affecting the relationship” from just… normal family obligations, if that makes sense. And with friends, I suppose I may be a little less present sometimes, but I don’t know if they would say anything is different.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering very directly. I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it affecting relationships in a major way, at least not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that came on suddenly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel very wound up or overwhelmed, but “intense fear that came on suddenly” makes me think of something very dramatic, and I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike, sometimes I can get thrown off by an email or something unexpected at work, and then my thoughts start going, but that feels more like it builds from a worry rather than appearing out of nowhere. Sorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I guess I haven’t really noticed a sudden episode like that, at least not one I’d clearly identify that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about multiple things at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s like they pile on top of each other.\n\nAt first it was mostly my mother’s safety, and I could sort of tell myself, “Well, that makes sense, she’s my mother.” But over this past year it’s spread into all these different buckets—money, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter.\n\nAnd they don’t stay separate, either. I’ll be thinking about a work deadline, and then suddenly I remember a house repair, and then I’m worrying about money, and then I’m back to my mother again. It’s almost embarrassing, because sometimes it’s just one small thing, and my brain treats it like everything is about to unravel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying affects most areas of your life, such as work, relationships, and personal activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, especially at work. That’s the part that bothers me most, because I’ve always been pretty dependable at the library. I’m usually the person who catches the small things, keeps the collections details straight, remembers which committee needs what.\n\nLately I’ve missed these small but important details, and then I start worrying about the fact that I missed them, which probably doesn’t help. And there was this grant presentation—I had to hand off parts of it because I just sort of froze. That’s really not like me.\n\nI don’t know if it affects every single area equally. Like with relationships, I’m less sure. But work, definitely. And it makes me worry whether I’m still doing a good job leading my team, which feels awful to even say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… almost every day, I think. It’s not always the exact same worry, which is part of what makes it feel so exhausting.\n\nAt first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and I could almost justify that to myself. But over the past year it’s spread into finances, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things with the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and then one little thing can set off this whole chain. Like I’ll notice a bill on the counter, and suddenly I’m thinking about the roof, and work coverage, and whether I should have called my mother earlier.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most days. Even when there isn’t one big obvious thing happening, my mind seems to find something to pull at.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure what counts as one, honestly. And sometimes everything is technically fine, like the bills are paid or work is moving along, but one small thing can still make me feel like it’s all about to unravel.\n\nI’ll think, “Why am I worrying right now? Nothing is happening.” But then I’ll find something—an email, a repair I haven’t scheduled, something someone said—and it starts up again.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change with friends or family in a clear way.\n\nI mean, with family it’s hard to separate what’s “worry affecting the relationship” from just… normal family obligations, if that makes sense. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel very wound up or overwhelmed, but “intense fear that came on suddenly” makes me think of something very dramatic, and I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike, sometimes I can get thrown off by an email or something unexpected at work, and then my thoughts start going, but that feels more like it builds from a worry rather than appearing out of nowhere. Sorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I guess I haven’t really noticed a sudden episode like that, at least not one I’d clearly identify that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about multiple things at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s like they pile on top of each other.\n\nAt first it was mostly my mother’s safety, and I could sort of tell myself, “Well, that makes sense, she’s my mother.” But over this past year it’s spread into all these different buckets—money, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter.\n\nAnd they don’t stay separate, either. I’ll be thinking about a work deadline, and then suddenly I remember a house repair, and then I’m worrying about money, and then I’m back to my mother again.
