Once a worry starts, it’s like I can’t put it back on the shelf in my mind.\n\nIt might be my mother getting up at night, or a grant report at work, or just wondering if I missed something important in a conversation. I’ll make lists, I’ll try to redirect myself, I’ll tell myself, “You’ve already thought this through,” but it just keeps coming back around. Sometimes I’ll be doing something completely different and realize I’m still mentally checking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about everyday, routine things like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. It used to be almost entirely about my mother—whether she was safe, whether I’d missed something she needed—but over this past year it’s sort of… spread.\n\nNow it’s finances, decisions at work with staff, deadlines, the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter. Even small things can feel like they’re about to unravel. Like one email or one bill isn’t just that one thing, it turns into this whole chain of “what if I’ve missed something?” or “what if this causes a bigger problem?” And then I’m off thinking about ten other things, which is probably not what you asked exactly, but yes, it’s everyday things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I keep wanting to say it’s not catastrophic, because I’m still going to work and getting things done, mostly. But it is starting to show up in ways that scare me a little.\n\nAt the library I’ve missed some small details—nothing huge, but things I normally catch. And there was a grant presentation recently where I just… froze. I had to hand parts of it off to someone else, which was embarrassing, and not really like me. I kept thinking afterward about whether everyone noticed, and whether I can still lead the team properly.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering a bit. But yes, it’s interfering. And it’s distressing because my work has always been the place where I felt competent.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things even when everything is going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s the frustrating part, because even when there isn’t an actual crisis, my mind sort of goes looking for one.\n\nOver this past year it’s spread from worrying mostly about my mother’s safety to all these other things—money, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. And sometimes everything is technically fine, like the bills are paid or work is moving along, but one small thing can still make me feel like it’s all about to unravel.\n\nI’ll think, “Why am I worrying right now? Nothing is happening.” But then I’ll find something—an email, a repair I haven’t scheduled, something someone said—and it starts up again.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change with friends or family in a clear way.\n\nI mean, with family it’s hard to separate what’s “worry affecting the relationship” from just… normal family obligations, if that makes sense. And with friends, I suppose I may be a little less present sometimes, but I don’t know if they would say anything is different.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering very directly. I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it affecting relationships in a major way, at least not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that came on suddenly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel very wound up or overwhelmed, but “intense fear that came on suddenly” makes me think of something very dramatic, and I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike, sometimes I can get thrown off by an email or something unexpected at work, and then my thoughts start going, but that feels more like it builds from a worry rather than appearing out of nowhere. Sorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I guess I haven’t really noticed a sudden episode like that, at least not one I’d clearly identify that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about multiple things at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s like they pile on top of each other.\n\nAt first it was mostly my mother’s safety, and I could sort of tell myself, “Well, that makes sense, she’s my mother.” But over this past year it’s spread into all these different buckets—money, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter.\n\nAnd they don’t stay separate, either. I’ll be thinking about a work deadline, and then suddenly I remember a house repair, and then I’m worrying about money, and then I’m back to my mother again. It’s almost embarrassing, because sometimes it’s just one small thing, and my brain treats it like everything is about to unravel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying affects most areas of your life, such as work, relationships, and personal activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, especially at work. That’s the part that bothers me most, because I’ve always been pretty dependable at the library. I’m usually the person who catches the small things, keeps the collections details straight, remembers which committee needs what.\n\nLately I’ve missed these small but important details, and then I start worrying about the fact that I missed them, which probably doesn’t help. And there was this grant presentation—I had to hand off parts of it because I just sort of froze. That’s really not like me.\n\nI don’t know if it affects every single area equally. Like with relationships, I’m less sure. But work, definitely. And it makes me worry whether I’m still doing a good job leading my team, which feels awful to even say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… almost every day, I think. It’s not always the exact same worry, which is part of what makes it feel so exhausting.\n\nAt first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and I could almost justify that to myself. But over the past year it’s spread into finances, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things with the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and then one little thing can set off this whole chain. Like I’ll notice a bill on the counter, and suddenly I’m thinking about the roof, and work coverage, and whether I should have called my mother earlier.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most days. Even when there isn’t one big obvious thing happening, my mind seems to find something to pull at.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure what counts as one, honestly. I’ve heard people describe them in different ways, and sometimes I think, well, everyone uses that phrase now—like at work someone will say they “had a panic attack” over a printer jam, which is not the same thing, I know.\n\nI don’t think I’ve had something I’d clearly call a panic attack, though. I get very worked up and overwhelmed, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the main reason I came in, honestly. I keep coming back to it, like when a book is mis-shelved and you know it’s somewhere in the stacks but you can’t stop looking for it.\n\nSorry, that’s a library comparison, but… yes. Most of the time, it feels like I can’t control it the way I should be able to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is excessive, considering the actual situations you're worrying about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is excessive, even though part of me keeps trying to argue that it’s all “reasonable.” I mean, my mother’s safety is a real concern, and work deadlines are real, and bills are real. So yes, when I step back, the amount of worry is bigger than the actual situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about the same things or does it shift between different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It shifts, definitely. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s… not usually just one specific problem anymore. I mean, it starts that way sometimes — like I’ll be thinking about my mother’s safety, or a bill, or some staff decision at work — but then it sort of spreads.

Over the past year it’s gone from being mostly about my mother to almost everything: finances, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. And then something tiny, like remembering a loose end at work while I’m doing something completely unrelated, can make it feel like everything is about to unravel.

So I guess it’s both? It attaches to specific things, but underneath it there’s this broader anxious feeling that just keeps looking for somewhere to land.

Sorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I guess I haven’t really noticed a sudden episode like that, at least not one I’d clearly identify that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about multiple things at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s like they pile on top of each other.\n\nAt first it was mostly my mother’s safety, and I could sort of tell myself, “Well, that makes sense, she’s my mother.” But over this past year it’s spread into all these different buckets—money, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m doing enough as a daughter.\n\nAnd they don’t stay separate, either. I’ll be thinking about a work deadline, and then suddenly I remember a house repair, and then I’m worrying about money, and then I’m back to my mother again. It’s almost embarrassing, because sometimes it’s just one small thing, and my brain treats it like everything is about to unravel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying affects most areas of your life, such as work, relationships, and personal activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, especially at work. That’s the part that bothers me most, because I’ve always been pretty dependable at the library. I’m usually the person who catches the small things, keeps the collections details straight, remembers which committee needs what.\n\nLately I’ve missed these small but important details, and then I start worrying about the fact that I missed them, which probably doesn’t help. And there was this grant presentation—I had to hand off parts of it because I just sort of froze. That’s really not like me.\n\nI don’t know if it affects every single area equally. Like with relationships, I’m less sure. But work, definitely. And it makes me worry whether I’m still doing a good job leading my team, which feels awful to even say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… almost every day, I think. It’s not always the exact same worry, which is part of what makes it feel so exhausting.\n\nAt first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and I could almost justify that to myself. But over the past year it’s spread into finances, staffing decisions at work, deadlines, things with the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and then one little thing can set off this whole chain. Like I’ll notice a bill on the counter, and suddenly I’m thinking about the roof, and work coverage, and whether I should have called my mother earlier.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most days. Even when there isn’t one big obvious thing happening, my mind seems to find something to pull at.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure what counts as one, honestly. I’ve heard people describe them in different ways, and sometimes I think, well, everyone uses that phrase now—like at work someone will say they “had a panic attack” over a printer jam, which is not the same thing, I know.\n\nI don’t think I’ve had something I’d clearly call a panic attack, though. I get very worked up and overwhelmed, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the main reason I came in, honestly. Once a worry starts, I can’t seem to put it away.\n\nIt can be about my mother getting up at night, or some grant report, or whether I missed some small thing at work—and I’ll try to make a list, or tell myself, “Okay, you’ve handled this,” but it doesn’t really stop. I keep coming back to it, like when a book is mis-shelved and you know it’s somewhere in the stacks but you can’t stop looking for it.\n\nSorry, that’s a library comparison, but… yes. Most of the time, it feels like I can’t control it the way I should be able to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is excessive, considering the actual situations you're worrying about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is excessive, even though part of me keeps trying to argue that it’s all “reasonable.” I mean, my mother’s safety is a real concern, and work deadlines are real, and bills are real. It’s not like I’m inventing things out of nowhere.\n\nBut over this past year it’s spread so much. It used to be mostly about my mother, and now it’s finances, staff decisions, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and then something small, like noticing an email I forgot to answer, can make it feel like everything is about to unravel. So yes, when I step back, the amount of worry is bigger than the actual situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about the same things or does it shift between different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It shifts, definitely. It used to be mostly my mother—whether she was safe, whether I should be doing more, that sort of thing. But over this past year it’s kind of… branched out.\n\nNow it can be money, or a staff decision at work, or a deadline, or something with the house. And then I’ll be rinsing a mug or shelving something in my head—sorry, that’s not quite the point—and suddenly I’m thinking, “Did I answer that email?” and it feels like that one small thing means everything is about to unravel.\n\nSo yes, it moves around between different parts of my life. It doesn’t stay in one lane anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stop worrying once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s… that’s really the thing that makes it feel unmanageable.\n\nOnce it starts, I can’t seem to stop it or put it away. Like if I start thinking about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I have due, or whether I missed some detail at work, it just keeps circling. I’ll make lists, or try to redirect myself, or tell myself I’ll deal with it in the morning, but it doesn’t really stay put.\n\nIt’s like I can’t shelve it properly in my mind, if that makes sense. It keeps sliding back out.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about a specific problem or issue, or is it more of a general feeling of anxiety that isn't focused on one particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s… not usually just one specific problem anymore. I mean, it starts that way sometimes — like I’ll be thinking about my mother’s safety, or a bill, or some staff decision at work — but then it sort of spreads.\n\nOver the past year it’s gone from being mostly about my mother to almost everything: finances, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. And then something tiny, like remembering a loose end at work while I’m doing something completely unrelated, can make it feel like everything is about to unravel.\n\nSo I guess it’s both? It attaches to specific things, but underneath it there’s this broader anxious feeling that just keeps looking for somewhere to land.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
